### Page 31

31
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?31
AI spending only pays off if the economy generates enough 
revenue from it, and does so quickly enough, to cover the cost of 
the capital deployed (especially data centers). Given the sheer size 
of the current pipeline, and anxiety about how much of it moves 
from plans to completed projects, we expect growing investor 
pressure to see that revenues do in fact materialize.
To understand the challenge, consider what it takes to break even 
or pay for itself year to year, including the cost of investor capital. 
A gigawatt of AI data center capacity costs roughly USD 40 billion. 
To cover its cost of capital, a 1GW facility must generate about 
USD 11 billion in revenue per year.
26
  The ~129GW of global frontier 
data center capacity expected by 2029 would therefore need to 
generate about USD 1.5 trillion a year in compute revenue, largely 
sourced from the application companies that must rent computing 
power to produce products and services that are consumed by 
enterprises, consumers and governments. These companies, 
in particular the private model labs, likely need to earn 50% 
margins above their computing costs. That means end-customer 
AI spending would need to total roughly twice that amount, about 
USD 3 trillion per year. 
So how much revenue could the AI ecosystem earn in the early 
2030s? Under reasonable assumptions, we estimate:
• Enterprises could spend about USD 2.5 trillion a year on AI 
through employee AI tools and agents.
• Consumer sectors could add about USD 750 billion in revenue 
through advertising and subscriptions.
• Governments could add another USD 400 billion.
27

That gets AI revenue to about USD ~3.5 trillion, about USD 700 
billion above the breakeven rate needed for the data centers to 
pay for themselves year to year. That surplus represents returns 
for investors in the compute and application layer. It may seem 
sizable, but it is based on an addressable market that is merely an 
estimate and far from assured.
A closely watched risk is whether a large share of AI-driven 
revenue, especially from large enterprises, comes from job 
displacement—replacing workers, rather than creating new 
How many trillions  
does AI need to deliver? 
demand. If half of potential enterprise AI spending replaces roles 
that would otherwise be filled, and AI costs about half as much 
as the worker it replaces, AI would absorb roughly 24 million 
knowledge worker jobs worldwide over five years, or about 1.3% 
of the global knowledge workforce each year. That is disruptive, 
but it is about a third of the pace at which U.S. manufacturing shed 
its workforce in the 2000s (~3.4% a year) amid factory automation 
and China’s entry into the World Trade Organization. Much of the 
AI adjustment could come through roles left unfilled rather than 
layoffs. The bigger risk is concentration: Displacement that falls 
heavily on specific occupations or regions could test political limits 
well before the aggregate numbers do.
28

To illustrate, if two-thirds of those displaced roles were in the 
United States, the U.S. would absorb roughly 16 million workers 
over five years, or about 3 million a year. That is roughly 2% of 
payrolls annually, more than four times the pace of the 2000s 
manufacturing decline. It would approach the rate of job loss 
during the Great Recession, only sustained for five years rather 
than two. Even if most of AI job displacement avoids layoffs, it 
would at the very least eliminate  the economy’s normal annual 
job growth. As the midterm election results suggest, it seems very 
unlikely that there would be political tolerance of this outcome. 
J.P. Morgan Asset Management’s analysis of AI’s impact on the 
economy suggests that job disruption is more likely to occur within 
jobs rather than eliminate jobs outright. After all, every job is 
ultimately a collection of tasks, and to date, AI can automate or 
augment only a portion of them. Moreover, while we anticipate AI 
diffusion across the economy will be profound, it will take time, 
and adoption is expected to be uneven and gradual. In our view,

this steady uptake should help limit the risk of widespread
job displacement.
Turning back to the economics of the AI build-out: The gap
between the revenue required for the AI ecosystem to break even

and the potential revenue from sources such as enterprises and
consumers is a critical variable. When the potential market is far
larger than what the AI ecosystem needs to break even, every new
data center looks like a solid investment. But as the infrastructure
pipeline grows toward the size of the addressable AI market, the
gap shrinks, and so do the incentives to keep building.
We believe the infrastructure pipeline is entering a more
selective phase. First, the addressable market is still very much

debated, especially after frontier model lab revenues decelerated
recently. Second, bond markets have started to balk at excessive
leverage and aggressive infrastructure plans from smaller AI
players. Finally, overbuilding is often how capital investment
booms end. The railroads and the internet were both tremendously
productive innovations. But far too much track and fiber were laid
before demand arrived. As a result, capital markets suffered severe

losses. In the AI cycle, though, more restrictions around data center
development reduce the risk that capacity exceeds demand.
Going forward, companies will find it difficult to convince
investors that they need additional data center capacity beyond
the current pipeline especially in light of the likelihood of a
more onerous regulatory environment. Therefore, value may
increasingly accrue to companies that control the crucial
bottlenecks, or have the pricing power to work through constraints

such as political pressure, regulatory shifts, power availability, grid
access, permitting speed and financing.

### Page 33

33
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?33
As even casual observers of the AI story understand, financing will be key to
how the plot unfolds. As a result, before you underwrite the equity at any layer
of the AI ecosystem, you should be comfortable with the debt on a company’s
balance sheet. (Another lesson from Economics 101: In a debt default, equity
value disappears.)
Some AI companies have recently hit the limits of what public debt markets

will tolerate. Consider the example of unsecured bonds from CoreWeave,
a “neocloud” (typically highly leveraged providers that rent out their compute
capacity). Their bonds trade at distressed level spreads well over 1,000bps.
Does this signal the beginning of the end of the AI boom?  No, but it does
suggest a different opportunity set within public and private markets.
Although AI debt is growing (AI-related issuance will top USD 300 billion this

year), debt growth as a share of GDP is still much lower than previous historical
leverage cycles and more political hurdles to data center development could
also result in a slower pace of leverage growth.

### Page 34

34
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?
Today, most of the AI debt is issued by investment grade borrowers with some of the
strongest balance sheets in the world. The hyperscalers as a whole (Alphabet, Amazon,
Microsoft, Oracle) carry interest coverage above 50x, compared with ~9x for the
median investment-grade issuer. Oracle is clearly an outlier. We still see opportunity in
some investment grade hyperscaler debt.
New financing structures have drawn scrutiny, though, because they can obscure who

owns the debt and what collateral sits behind it. Case in point: ~USD 165 billion in off-
balance-sheet from Nvidia support roughly 6.5GW of planned compute capacity from new
model providers.
29
Nvidia aims to cultivate a customer base beyond the hyperscalers and
frontier model labs. In principle, this should reduce concentration risk
in the AI ecosystem. But off-balance-sheet structures add to the opacity of the system
and are likely raising the cost of capital.

Artificial intelligence has shifted the technology sector from an asset-light to an asset-
heavy business model. A decade ago, the largest cloud companies reinvested about 7%
of their revenue in capital spending. This year, analysts’ consensus expects roughly 36%.
That is nearly the reinvestment rate of a regulated utility, and it absorbs almost all of the
cloud companies’ operating cash flow.
The further you move from the hyperscalers, the more asset-heavy the business model

becomes. Neoclouds such as CoreWeave spend close to USD 3 in capex for every dollar
of revenue, and they fund the difference with debt. The demand for capital is also growing
in the power sector, where U.S. utilities plan nearly USD 1.5 trillion of investment over the
next five years.
30
Today the assets in focus are GPUs and data centers. Tomorrow they could be
autonomous vehicles and robots. Either way, asset-heavy models need financing. We have

been exploring opportunities to provide that financing through private markets to data
center developers and neoclouds that are struggling to raise capital in public markets on
attractive terms. This is a riskier proposition, which comes with commensurately higher
potential returns.

### Page 35

WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?35
What you must believe to own
the infrastructure layer
The infrastructure layer (semiconductors, memory, electrical
equipment, power generation and construction) gets paid on
delivery. Its revenue is a function of gigawatts built.
To allocate your capital to AI infrastructure firms, you need to
believe the build-out will be larger than what investors have priced
into the stocks. That has so far proved true. Wall Street analysts

have underestimated hyperscaler capex growth by an average of
50 percentage points in each of the last three years.
However, as we have explained, we believe we are reaching the
limits of upward revisions to the infrastructure layer, even if
demand for compute seems to be outstripping physical capacity.
To believe that the equity of the infrastructure layer can
outperform from here, you also probably need to believe that
the political restrictions on data center development are less

restrictive than what is currently priced into markets. At the time
of writing, semiconductor stocks were already pricing in between
a 20% and 40% decline in earnings. Further, an ever-larger fleet
of AI data centers would require consistent replenishment of
semiconductors to support revenues. Finally, sovereign demand
for AI capacity may result in a global data center fleet built for
redundancy and security, not capital efficiency.
Our base case scenario assumes decelerating capex growth,

but that comes with a silver lining. A slower build preserves
scarcity, and scarcity may be more profitable for the rest of the AI
ecosystem, at least in the near term. Limiting data center supply
could help maintain pricing power for both computing power and
intelligence itself, even if it limits the potential for AI disruption.
Focus on the
hyperscaler elite
We are more bullish about the prospects for hyperscalers. Looking
across the public market AI ecosystem, we think the group may be

most deserving of your capital today.
The hyperscalers could benefit whether compute is abundant or
scarce, cheap or expensive. They draw strength from the reach of
their businesses: They own the data centers, run the clouds and
sell the AI applications (search, advertising, productivity software
and consumer products). When compute is scarce, they benefit by
charging higher rents for their capacity. When compute is more
plentiful, and thus less costly, hyperscalers’  own application

businesses benefit from lower prices.
We can also see a clear path to a return on their AI investments.
Sales growth in core cloud businesses is accelerating. Meta’s and
Alphabet’s investments in AI-driven advertising  are starting to pay
off. Hyperscaler balance sheets could bend, but we do not believe
they will break. Recent disappointing earnings reports are also
providing an attractive entry point for investors.
A moderating pace of infrastructure build, which we now expect,

would also ease pressure on hyperscalers’ free cash flow. Finally,
the hyperscalers now trade at attractive prices in both the debt
and equity markets. As an aggregate, their stocks trade at a very
slight P/E premium to the market (well below their own long
run averages) and price-to-earnings growth ratios suggest that
markets aren’t giving them very much credit for continued growth.
Heading into 2027, we believe hyperscalers present the best
risk-adjusted returns for your public equity capital.

### Page 37

37
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?37
The application layer sits at the top of the AI stack: the frontier 
labs, enterprise AI platforms and vertical software companies 
that turn tokens into products customers buy. Companies in the 
application layer operate in a variety of sectors, from coding 
and customer service to healthcare and law. Their revenue is a 
function of tokens consumed or, increasingly, outcomes delivered. 
The application layer’s main cost is the compute it rents.
Frontier labs and other application firms sell “intelligence” (the 
ability to reason and work toward a goal) for which they can now 
charge a premium price. Today, frontier labs in the application 
layer earn 60%–90% gross margins.
31
Markets consistently question whether the frontier labs’ 
exceptional profit margins are sustainable. These labs could face 
growing competition from China-origin open-weight models, which 
now account for ~80% of tokens generated on platforms such as 
OpenRouter, an AI platform/service provider. Enterprises are also 
keen to limit the cost of their employees’ token usage, which could 
impinge on frontier lab revenue.
32

To be bullish about the application layer, you need to believe in 
some combination of the following scenarios: 
•  Model quality matters. Customers don’t choose models 
on price alone. They weigh cost against quality, reliability, 
latency (essentially how long it takes for a model to respond 
to a query) and how well a model aligns with a customer’s 
workflows. Open-weight models will certainly improve, but 
we think frontier models will command a premium for the 
foreseeable future. Indeed, they still account for over 90%  
of enterprise token spending. 
• Pricing could move from tokens to outcomes.  
Under usage-based pricing, revenue is tied to a unit that 
should keep getting cheaper. Under outcome-based pricing, 
revenue is tied to the value of the work. As a result, falling 
token costs widen margins without reducing revenue. 
• Demand extends meaningfully beyond today’s base. Enterprise 
adoption of agentic workflows, household adoption of 
consumer agents and physical world AI applications such 
as robots begin to take hold. AI’s promised future starts to 
become real.
The history of value creation favors the “second wave” companies 
in a capex boom, the firms that build on the early infrastructure. 
That is perhaps the most persuasive argument for the value of the 
application layer. The rise of railroads in the United States enabled 
the launch of Sears department stores. Electricity set the stage 
for Hollywood entertainment. The internet enabled e-commerce 
and the app store. Another lesson from history is that innovation 
cycles lead to increased demand in other sectors of the economy. 
Today, we believe that demand for experiences, travel, luxury,  
wellness, longevity and sports could all increase as AI diffuses. 
At the beginning of the electricity and computer era, public 
markets reflected the incumbents. But within 30 years, nearly half 
of the 10 largest publicly traded stocks in the market were native 
to those technologies. We believe the same pattern will prevail 
in the AI era. Consider exposure in today’s private markets to the 
potential public giants of tomorrow.
What you must believe 
to invest your capital in  
the application layer

2027 ANNUAL OUTLOOK
Conclusion
A focus on your capital—demand for capital, cost of capital—will reverberate
through markets in the coming year. Do you believe in the AI boom enough to
provide the financing that could bridge the gap between capital investment
and durable revenue? Will you bet on the beneficiaries of security spending?
Will you use the potential returns from higher interest rates to lower risk
within your portfolio while maintaining your previous return target? Cash may

even be a slightly more attractive proposition.
As always, you’ll want to build a portfolio that is sturdy enough, and flexible
enough, to help you achieve your long-term goals. We are here to help assess
the investment potential and challenges across the financial landscape.
In 2027 and beyond, we aim to help ensure you charge the right price
for your capital.

Indices are shown for illustrative purposes only. An index
is unmanaged, is not an investment product and may not
be considered for direct investment. Index returns do not
reflect the deduction of any fees or expenses and assume
reinvestment of dividends and interest. All indices are
denominated in U.S. dollars unless noted otherwise. Indices
are an inherently weak predictive or comparative tool and
provide a hypothetical representation for use as a benchmark.

5-year / 5-year forward inflation breakeven rate (5y5y): A
market-based measure of expected average inflation over the
five-year period beginning five years from today, derived from
nominal and inflation-protected government bond yields.
Agentic AI / agentic models: Artificial intelligence systems
designed to plan and carry out multi-step tasks with
limited human intervention, often by using tools, retrieving
information and taking actions within defined workflows.

AI application layer: The part of the AI ecosystem
that converts models and tokens into products or
services customers buy, including frontier labs,
enterprise platforms, agents and vertical software.
AI compute layer: The part of the AI ecosystem that supplies and
rents the computing capacity used to train and run AI models,
including chips, servers, cloud infrastructure and related services.
AI infrastructure layer: The physical foundation
of the AI ecosystem, including semiconductors,

memory, networking, electrical equipment, power
generation, data centers and construction.
Alignment risk: The investment risk that a country,
company or asset becomes disadvantaged as geopolitical
interests, standards, trade relationships or financial
systems diverge across competing blocs.
Alternative assets: Investment categories outside
traditional public equities and core fixed income,
including private equity, private credit, hedge funds,
infrastructure, real estate and commodities.

Basis point (bp or bps): One one-hundredth of a percentage
point. For example, 100 basis points equal 1 percentage point.
Bottleneck basket: A thematic group of companies that
make or control scarce physical inputs needed for economic
and national security, such as semiconductors, commodities,
power generation equipment and gas turbines.
BRIC countries: Brazil, Russia, India and China,
a grouping of large emerging economies.
Business development company (BDC): A publicly traded or non-

traded investment company that primarily lends to or invests in
small and middle-market businesses, often through private credit.
Capital expenditure (capex): Spending to acquire, build or
upgrade long-lived assets such as data centers, machinery,
power infrastructure and other property or equipment.
Carry: The income earned from holding an investment,
such as a bond coupon or credit spread, before
changes in market price and other costs.
Central bank policy rate: The short-term interest rate

set or targeted by a central bank to influence financial
conditions, economic activity and inflation.
Consumer Price Index (CPI): A measure of inflation that
tracks changes over time in prices paid by consumers
for a representative basket of goods and services.
Correlation: A statistical measure of how two variables
move in relation to one another. A correlation of 1 indicates
they move together, 0 indicates no consistent relationship
and -1 indicates they move in opposite directions.

Credit spread: The additional yield on a bond or loan relative
to a comparable government security or benchmark rate,
compensating investors for credit and liquidity risk.
Debt-to-GDP ratio: A measure of a country’s public debt
relative to its gross domestic product, commonly used to assess
the scale and sustainability of government indebtedness.
Developed markets (DM): Countries with advanced economies,
established financial markets and generally high income levels,

such as the United States, Japan and many European economies.
Direct lending: Private credit provided directly by non-
bank lenders to companies, often through negotiated
senior-secured loans to middle-market borrowers.
DEFINITIONS OF INDICES AND TERMS

### Page 44

Diversified hedge funds: Pooled investment strategies
that use multiple approaches, such as macro, relative value,
long/short and event-driven investing, to seek returns with
lower dependence on broad stock and bond markets.
Emerging markets (EM): Countries with developing
economies and financial markets that may offer higher
growth potential but generally involve greater economic,
political, currency and market risk than developed markets.
Federal funds rate: The interest rate at which U.S. banks lend

reserve balances to one another overnight; the Federal Reserve
sets a target range for this rate as its principal policy tool.
Free cash flow: Cash generated by a company after operating
expenses and capital expenditures, available for debt repayment,
dividends, share repurchases, acquisitions or reinvestment.
Frontier labs / model providers: Organizations that develop
and commercialize leading-edge artificial intelligence models,
often selling access through subscriptions, application

programming interfaces or usage-based pricing.
Gross margin: Revenue less the direct cost of providing
goods or services, expressed as a percentage of revenue.
High yield: Corporate debt rated below investment
grade, generally offering higher yields to compensate
investors for greater credit and default risk.
Hyperscalers: Large cloud-computing providers able to
deliver computing resources at a scale and speed that
supports major enterprises and internet services.

Inflation breakeven rate: The difference between the yields
of nominal and inflation-protected government bonds of
similar maturity, commonly interpreted as a market-based
measure of expected inflation plus an inflation risk premium.
Infrastructure: Long-lived physical assets and
systems that support economic activity, including
power generation and transmission, transportation,
communications and data center assets.
Interest coverage ratio: A measure of a borrower’s ability

to meet interest payments, typically calculated as earnings
before interest and taxes divided by interest expense.
Investment grade (IG): Bonds or borrowers rated at
or above the minimum investment grade threshold by
recognized credit-rating agencies, indicating comparatively
lower expected default risk than high yield debt.
Latency: The time required for an artificial intelligence
model or computing system to respond to a request.
Liquidity: The ease with which an asset can be bought or

sold without materially affecting its price. Private investments
generally offer less liquidity than publicly traded securities.
Loss-adjusted yield: An investment’s yield after
deducting expected credit losses, typically based on
assumptions for default rates and recovery values.
Nearshoring: Relocating production or services closer to a
company’s end market, often to neighboring or regionally aligned
countries, to reduce supply-chain risk and transportation costs.

Neocloud: A specialized cloud provider, often focused on
renting AI computing capacity and frequently relying more
heavily on external financing than established hyperscalers.
Nominal GDP: The value of goods and services
produced by an economy measured at current
prices, without adjusting for inflation.
Off-balance-sheet commitment: A contractual
obligation or financial exposure that is not recorded as
conventional debt on the sponsoring company’s balance

sheet, though it may still create economic risk.
Open-source model: An artificial intelligence model whose
underlying code, model weights or technical components are made
available for others to use, modify or deploy, subject to its license.
Outcome-based pricing: A pricing model in which customers
pay for the value or result delivered, rather than for the
number of users, software seats or tokens consumed.
Overnight index swap / overnight rate futures: Market

instruments used to express expectations for the future path of
short-term interest rates linked to an overnight benchmark.
DEFINITIONS OF INDICES AND TERMS
44

### Page 45

45
DEFINITIONS OF INDICES AND TERMS
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price
index: A broad measure of prices paid by U.S. consumers
for goods and services. The Federal Reserve uses the
PCE price index as its preferred inflation gauge.
Price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple: A valuation measure calculated
by dividing a company’s share price by its earnings per share.
Private credit: Privately negotiated lending by non-bank
investors to companies, typically offering less liquidity than

public bonds and often using floating interest rates.
Real assets: Investments tied to physical assets or resources,
including infrastructure, real estate and commodities, that
may provide income and potential sensitivity to inflation.
Real return: An investment return after adjusting for inflation.
Recovery rate: The share of principal and accrued value
that lenders recover after a borrower defaults.
Reserve currency: A currency widely held by
central banks and institutions and commonly used

in international trade, finance and payments.
S&P 500 Index: A market-capitalization-weighted
index of 500 leading U.S. companies, widely used as
a gauge of the U.S. large-cap equity market.
Security and Resiliency Initiative (SRI): JPMorganChase’s
USD 1.5 trillion, 10-year initiative to facilitate, finance
and invest in industries considered critical to economic
security and resiliency, including an initial USD 10
billion of direct investments in select companies.

Secured debt: Borrowing backed by specified collateral
that lenders may claim if the borrower fails to repay.
Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR): A broad
measure of the cost of borrowing cash overnight
collateralized by U.S. Treasury securities and a widely
used reference rate for U.S. dollar financing.
Shadow default rate: A broader measure of credit stress
that goes beyond reported payment defaults to include
indicators such as non-accruals, distressed payment-

in-kind arrangements, material maturity extensions,
foreclosures and deeply discounted loan marks.
Sovereign credit: Debt issued or guaranteed by
a national government, whose risk reflects the
sovereign’s fiscal position, economic capacity, policy
credibility and ability and willingness to repay.
Take-or-pay contract: An agreement requiring a customer
to pay for a minimum amount of capacity, goods or
services whether or not the customer uses them.

Taxable-equivalent yield: The pretax yield a taxable bond
would need to provide to match the after-tax yield of a tax-
exempt bond, based on an investor’s applicable tax rate.
Token: A basic unit of text or data processed by an artificial
intelligence model. Model usage and pricing are often measured
by the number of input, output and reasoning tokens consumed.
U.S. Treasury yield: The market interest rate on
debt issued by the U.S. government. Treasury yields

serve as benchmarks for borrowing costs and
investor expectations across financial markets.
Unsecured debt: Borrowing that is not backed by specific
collateral, leaving lenders dependent on the issuer’s
general creditworthiness and claim on its assets.
USMCA: The United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement,
the trade agreement governing economic relations
among the three countries and replacing the
North American Free Trade Agreement.
Usage-based pricing: A pricing model in which customer

charges rise or fall with measured consumption, such as tokens
processed, computing capacity used or transactions completed.
Vertical software: Software designed for the workflows
and specialized requirements of a specific industry or
profession, such as healthcare, law or financial services.

UNDERSTANDING LONG-TERM ESTIMATES
Our investment management research incorporates our
proprietary projections of the returns and volatility of each asset
class over the long term, as well as estimates of the correlations
among asset classes. Clearly, financial firms cannot predict how
markets will perform in the future. But we do believe that by
analyzing current economic and market conditions and historical
market trends, and then, most critically, making projections

of future economic growth, inflation and real yields for each
country, we can estimate the long-term performance for an entire
asset class, given current and our estimated equilibrium levels.
The “equilibrium” level shows the average or central tendency
of a market or macroeconomic variable such as yield or credit
spread that is expected to prevail over the long term, because
the level represents the value inherent in a given market. The
return assumptions are based on our proprietary process of

using a building block approach for each of the asset classes. For
instance, the building blocks for equity consist of our projections
on inflation, real earnings growth, dividend yield and the impact
of valuations. The building blocks for fixed income consist of
our projections for future yields and the change in bond prices.
The estimates for alternatives are driven by our historical
analysis and judgment about the relationship to public markets.

It is possible—indeed, probable—that actual returns will vary
considerably from this expectation, even for a number of years.
But we believe that market returns will always at some point
return to the equilibrium trend. We further believe that these
kinds of forward-looking assessments are far more accurate
than historical trends in deciding what asset class performance
will be, and how best to determine an optimal asset mix.
In reviewing this material, please understand that all references

to expected return are not promises, or even estimates, of actual
returns one may achieve. The assumptions are not based on
specific products and do not reflect fees, such as investment
management fees, oversight fees, transaction costs or other
expenses that could reduce return. They simply show what the
long-term return should be, according to our best estimates
of current and equilibrium conditions. Also note that actual
performance may be affected by the expertise of the person who

actually manages these investments, both in picking individual
securities and possibly adjusting the mix periodically to take
advantage of asset class undervaluations and overvaluations
caused by market trends. For the purpose of this analysis volatility
is defined as a statistical measure of the dispersion of return for
a given allocation and is measured as the standard deviation of
the allocation’s arithmetic return. The Sharpe ratio is a return/

risk measure, where the return (the numerator) is defined as the
incremental annual return of an investment over the risk-free
rate. Risk (the denominator) is defined as the standard deviation
(volatility) of the allocation’s return less the risk-free rate. The risk-
free rate utilized is J.P. Morgan’s long-term assumption for Cash.
Correlation is a statistical measure of the degree to which
the movements of two variables, in this case asset class

returns, are related. Correlation can range from -1 to 1 with
1 indicating that the returns of two assets move directionally
in concert with one another, i.e. they behave in the same
way during the same time. A correlation of 0 indicates
that the returns move independently of each other and
-1 indicates that they move in the opposite direction.

IMPORTANT INFORMATION
Key risks
Alternative investments: Investing in alternative assets involves
higher risks than traditional investments, including, without
limitation, limited liquidity, and is suitable only for investors with
sufficient knowledge and sophistication to evaluate the merits and
risks of such investments. Alternative investments should not be
deemed a complete investment program and distributions are not
guaranteed. They may not be tax efficient, and an investor should

consult with their tax professional prior to investing. Alternative
investments often have higher fees than traditional investments
and they may also be highly leveraged and engage in speculative
investment techniques, which can magnify the investment loss
or gain—including risk of loss of the entire investment. For
comprehensive details around unique set of risks for specific
alternative investments, please consult the offering memorandum.
Bonds: Bonds are subject to interest rate risk, credit and default risk

of the issuer. Bond prices generally fall when interest rates rise.
Commodities: Investments in commodities may have
greater volatility than investments in traditional securities.
The value of commodities may be affected by changes in
overall market movements, commodity index volatility,
changes in interest rates, or factors affecting a particular
industry or commodity, such as drought, floods, weather,
livestock disease, embargoes, tariffs and international

economic, political and regulatory developments. Investing in
commodities creates an opportunity for increased return but,
at the same time, creates the possibility for greater loss.
Diversification: Diversification and asset allocation
does not ensure a profit or protect against loss.
Equities: The price of equity securities may rise or fall due to
changes in the broad market or changes in a company’s financial
condition, sometimes rapidly or unpredictably. Equity securities

are subject to “stock market risk,” meaning that stock prices in
general may decline over short or extended periods of time.
Emerging markets: Investing in emerging markets involves
a greater degree of risk and increased volatility compared
to developed markets. Changes in currency exchange rates
and differences in accounting and taxation policies outside
the investor’s jurisdiction can raise or lower returns. Some
markets may not be as politically and economically stable, and

differences in taxation policies and legal systems outside the
investor’s jurisdiction may create additional risks. Investors
should carefully consider these risks and consult with financial
and legal advisors before investing in emerging markets.
Fixed income: Investing in fixed income products is
subject to certain risks, including interest rate, credit,
inflation, call, prepayment and reinvestment risk.
High yield bonds: High yield bonds are speculative noninvestment

grade bonds that have higher risk of default or other adverse
credit events which are appropriate for high risk investors only.
International investments: International investments may not
be suitable for all investors. International investing involves
a greater degree of risk and increased volatility. Changes
in currency exchange rates and differences in accounting
and taxation policies outside the U.S. can raise or lower
returns. Some overseas markets may not be as politically and

economically stable as the United States and other nations.
Investments in international markets can be more volatile.
Municipal Bonds: Investors should understand the potential
tax liabilities surrounding a municipal bond purchase. Certain
municipal bonds are federally taxed if the holder is subject to
alternative minimum tax. Capital gains, if any, are federally
taxable. The investor should note that the income from
tax-free municipal bond funds may be subject to state and

local taxation and the Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT).
Preferreds: Preferred investments share characteristics of
both stocks and bonds. Preferred securities are typically long-
dated securities with call protection that fall in between debt
and equity in the capital structure. Preferred securities carry
various risks and considerations which include concentration
risk; interest rate risk; lower credit ratings than individual
bonds; a lower claim to assets than a firm’s individual

bonds; higher yields due to these risk characteristics; and
callable implications, meaning the issuing company may
redeem the stock at a certain price after a certain date.
Small capitalization companies: Small capitalization companies
typically carry more risk than well-established blue-chip
companies since smaller companies can carry a higher degree of
market volatility than most large cap and/or blue-chip companies.

### Page 4

◆ The economy remains on the recovery track despite weakness in some
areas
Annualized real GDP growth in the second quarter was +1.1% QoQ, with growth
staying in positive territory for three straight quarters. Although personal
consumption exhibited weakness and capital investment declined, overall growth
was lifted by foreign demand. Exports, particularly of services, increased, and the
drop in imports of Middle Eastern crude oil also helped foreign demand turn positive.

◆ Housing starts are weak
Housing starts surged 18.6% YoY in June. They have continued to rise on a YoY
basis, but the jump was mainly due to a rebound from the slump in the same month
of the previous year. The recovery in the number of starts is actually moderate,
reflecting higher interest rates and soaring construction costs.
◆ Earthquake impact will need to be watched
Damage from the earthquake that occurred in Kumamoto Prefecture at the end of

July is weighing on economic activity in the affected areas. Although moves toward
recovery can be seen, with many companies having already resumed operations,
the impact could become prolonged in the following scenarios:
The first is if damage to capital stock such as factories and roads has been severe.
Some companies have sustained damage to production facilities, making an early
resumption of operations difficult in some cases.
The second is if a downturn in tourism demand occurs. According to some media

reports, there has been an uptick in cancellations of accommodation reservations in
Kumamoto Prefecture and neighboring prefectures since the earthquake. After the
2016 Kumamoto earthquake, the decline in travel demand from Japanese visitors
was short lived, but lower demand from foreign tourists persisted for a long period.
Annualized real GDP growth was +1.1% quarter over quarter (QoQ) in Q2 2026

### Page 5

Monthly Report of Prospects for Japan's Economy August 2026
The Japan Research Institute, Limited
Exports and inbound demand remain solid despite weakness in some areas
◆ Goods exports are on the verge of picking up
Goods exports are treading water. A sharp rise in memory chip prices is among
factors pushing up export prices, resulting in record high levels of exports in value
terms recently. On the other hand, the increase in volume terms is limited. One

possible reason is that Japan's export competitiveness has declined in fields such as
semiconductors, where global demand is growing.
Looking ahead, exports are expected to pick up. Capital investment by chipmakers
is expanding worldwide, and this should lift exports of semiconductor manufacturing
equipment, an area in which Japan has strength. However, continued caution is
needed regarding the effects of prolonged turmoil in the Middle East. A global

slowdown due to elevated crude oil prices could weigh on exports.
◆ Reluctance to visit among Chinese travelers is weighing on demand for
travel to Japan
Inbound demand is currently soft. The Chinese government's advisory urging its
citizens to refrain from traveling to Japan is having an adverse impact. The number
of Chinese visitors to Japan has fallen to about half the level prior to the advisory.
Meanwhile, the number of tourists from countries other than China remains firm.

Inbound demand is expected to stay robust going forward. Continued increases in
tourists from countries other than China will remain a supporting factor. The
expectation that the yen will remain weak will also act to push up inbound
consumption. However, there are two downside risks going forward:
The first is that the Chinese government keeps the advisory in place for a
prolonged period. When China banned group travel to South Korea in 2017, tourist

numbers stayed low for two to three years.
The second is the impact of higher crude oil prices. Major airlines have embarked
on phased increases in fuel surcharges, which could weigh on inbound demand
during the summer.

### Page 6

Personal consumption is gradually recovering thanks to a favorable income environment
◆ Higher disposable incomes are supporting personal consumption
Personal consumption continues to gradually recover. While consumption of
non-durable goods remains weak, services consumption is firm, particularly in
amusement-related services. In addition, consumption of durable goods has
increased significantly. Temporary factors such as the abolition of the environmental

performance tax on automobiles (since April 2026) and the tightening of
energy-efficiency standards for air conditioners (effective April 2027) have also
contributed to this rise.
A favorable income environment can also be pointed to as a background factor
behind the recovery in personal consumption. YoY real disposable income growth has
generally been positive since mid-2024, and the pace of increase has recently
accelerated. Employee remuneration is rising, partly as a result of agreements made

during the shunto (spring wage negotiations), and net asset income is also increasing
at a faster clip as companies hike dividends in line with stock price rises.
◆ Stock price developments could be a source of concern for personal
consumption
Personal consumption is expected to continue its gradual recovery going forward. In
addition to the favorable income environment and government policy measures to
curb price increases, a recovery in consumer confidence driven by easing tensions in

the Middle East will also provide a tailwind. Recently, the Consumer Confidence Index
has been showing signs of bottoming out.
However, a reverse wealth effect stemming from falling stock prices is a potential
cause for concern. The Nikkei Stock Average, which reached a new high in June, has
been struggling to push higher since the start of July. Estimates suggest the wealth
effect is concentrated among elderly households, but in recent years working-age

households have also increased their holdings of equity-related assets, and the
positive wealth effect for them may have supported consumption to some extent. For
this reason, if stock prices were to fall significantly, there is a danger of downward
pressure on consumption across a wide range of households.

### Page 7

セン
To
◆ The unemployment rate has settled in the mid-2% range
While the employment situation is generally favorable, weakness can be seen in
some areas. The unemployment rate has remained in the mid-2% range since 2022.
The number of employed persons in non-manufacturing, where business conditions
are favorable, is holding firm, while in manufacturing the number remains at a level
below that seen before the COVID-19 pandemic.
In the background to the drop in manufacturing employment is a slump in

production activity. Manufacturing output has been shrinking due to the effects of
U.S. tariff policy, soaring raw material prices, and a decline in the international
competitiveness of Japanese firms. As a result, pressure to reduce headcounts has
been building. Even in non-manufacturing, some industries closely linked to
manufacturing, such as transportation and wholesale/retail, are also exhibiting
weakness in labor demand.
◆ The employment situation could remain fragile in the near term

Looking ahead at the employment situation in the short term, a sense of
stagnation may persist. The contraction in output in the manufacturing sector will
continue to weigh on labor demand. Although production activity has been brisker
recently, particularly in semiconductor-related industries, the employment-creation
capacity of such industries is small. The employment-inducement coefficient per unit
of new demand tends to be lower than in other sectors, meaning that the industries

lack the strength to serve as drivers of employment.
However, in the medium to long term, the unemployment rate is expected to see a
gradual decline. Demand for labor is projected to stay solid, especially in
face-to-face services such as medical/nursing care and accommodation and
eating/drinking services, but the number of people seeking employment from the
non-labor force population has declined significantly due to the expansion in elderly
and female workforce participation. With limited room for expansion in the labor

supply, the job market is expected to tighten further.

The government is curbing inflation
◆ Policy factors are pushing down the inflation rate
In June, core inflation (all items, less fresh food) was 1.6%, staying under 2%
for the fifth consecutive month. Food price inflation has slowed, and a major

reason for this is a fallback in rice prices, which soared last year. Gasoline
subsidies are also continuing to curtail energy price increases.
Inflation looks set to hover in the 2% range in the second half of 2026. Although
energy subsidies and other government measures will soften inflationary pressure
to some extent, with imported inflationary pressure building, prices are expected
to be pushed up as higher costs are passed through to selling prices. From

FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items will
serve to keep prices down, and inflation is expected to drop into the 1% range.
◆ The Bank of Japan (BOJ) will keep its policy rate unchanged
At its July monetary policy meeting, the BOJ decided to keep its policy rate
unchanged.
Long-term interest rates remain high. This reflects concerns about inflation
resulting from the increase in oil prices and expectations of an expansion in fiscal

expenditure.
The BOJ is expected to step up the pace of interest rate hikes while closely
monitoring the impact of higher rates on the real economy and financial/capital
markets. The next rate hike is expected to be in September.
Long-term interest rates look likely to rise gradually, reflecting heightened
anticipation of rate hikes by the BOJ and increased fiscal spending by the
government. If jitters about a deterioration in fiscal discipline resulting from such

measures as energy subsidies or the consumption tax cut take hold, there is also
a risk that long-term interest rates will rise.

◆ Surging crude oil prices have become a source of upward pressure on
prices
With crude oil prices having spiked against the backdrop of the worsening situation
in the Middle East, corporate input costs have risen sharply, and pressure for price
pass-through is intensifying. Looking at the Final Demand-Intermediate Demand
price indexes, the margin of price increases at the upstream stage far exceeds that at
the downstream stage, and price pass-through pressure is also substantial at the

downstream stage.
According to estimates based on past data, the impact of rising oil prices involves
a time lag. Specifically, the push-up effect on the consumer price inflation rate
increases for around 10 months and continues to exert influence for a long period
thereafter.
◆ Price pass-through willingness is also stronger than before
Notably, food product manufacturers are planning to raise prices for a huge
number of items during the summer and beyond. According to Teikoku Databank, the

number of items slated for price increases as of the end of July, when it conducted its
latest survey, was higher than in the May and June surveys.
If government subsidies for electricity/gas bills and gasoline prices are scaled back
or ended, both energy and food prices will increase concurrently, and the consumer
price inflation rate may rise again.
Attention also needs to be paid to the possibility that price pass-through driven by
elevated oil prices will become prolonged. In the 2020s, the rate of price increases in

transactions between companies has been higher than in the 2010s, and firms'
medium-term outlooks for selling prices have also shifted upward. Events that trigger
cost increases, such as heightened geopolitical tensions, have been occurring
repeatedly, and companies may be more willing to pass through higher costs to
prices than they were before.

### Page 10

The economy is expected to continue to recover, but downside risks are present
◆ The economy is projected to stay on the recovery track
The Japanese economy should continue its gradual recovery. WTI crude oil
futures prices are expected to fall moderately going forward.
In the corporate sector, government support for investment is providing a tailwind,
and demand for investment for labor saving and digital transformation remains
robust. In addition, rising global demand for AI-related goods should support exports.

Supply chain disruptions resulting from the deterioration in the Middle East situation
will begin to ease, and corporate production activity is expected to gradually recover.
In the household sector, inflationary pressure is expected to strengthen from the
summer onward due to higher crude oil prices. In response, the government will
probably ease the inflationary pressure through measures such as energy subsidies.
While the pace of increase in personal consumption is likely to slow somewhat,

consumption is expected to remain resilient amid continued high wage growth. From
FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items should also
support personal consumption.
◆ The Middle East situation and rising interest rates constitute risks
Real GDP growth is projected to remain solid, registering +0.8% in FY2026 and
+0.9% in FY2027.
However, if the situation in the Middle East worsens again or becomes prolonged,
there is a risk that downward pressure on the economy will intensify, mainly through

three channels: 1) weaker exports, 2) raw material supply constraints, and 3) rising
prices. In addition, if market concerns about the fiscal stance of the Takaichi
government grow, the yen could weaken further, and long-term interest rates could
rise, potentially forcing the scaling-back or scrapping of expansionary policies such
as investment support measures, energy subsidies, and lower consumption tax.
Monthly Report of Prospects for Japan's Economy August 2026

The Japan Research Institute, Limited- ８ -

Document 417: THINK Ahead_ Your questions, answered

Source PDF: all report pdf/THINK Ahead_ Your questions, answered.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-20

### Page 1

Our Q&A explores where ING’s Global Outlook diﬀers from
market expectations, from the path for interest rates and
inﬂation to US debt, European ﬁscal risks, wages and gold

Carsten Brzeski, Michiel Tukker, Peter Vanden Houte, James
Knightley and Ewa Manthey
Our Q&A examines how ING’s Global Outlook diﬀers from market expectations
and where we see the biggest disconnects
What is the biggest disconnect today
between what the market is pricing and
ING’s Global Outlook?
Our base case scenario is more dovish and benign than markets
are currently pricing in. We expect oil prices to fall further, easing
inﬂationary pressures and allowing central banks to stop hiking
rates after a ﬁnal rate hike in December.
Due to weaker household balance sheets, we see only limited pass-
through from higher energy prices to core inﬂation, with companies
instead having to squeeze proﬁt margins. In this scenario, major

### Page 2

central banks could even start considering rate cuts towards the
end of 2027.
Is it normal for markets to be pricing in
virtually no rate cuts? Beyond 12 months,
interest rate expectations are virtually ﬂat.
How does that compare to historic rate hike
cycles?
Compared to today’s curve, in 2008 and early 2023 markets priced
in a much larger decline in rates beyond the near-term peak. The
current curve is much more similar in shape to the 2007 hiking

cycle.
The current curve is more similar to the
2007 hiking cycle than the one in 2023
Based on these prior hiking episodes, we foresee two conditions
that would warrant a more humped yield curve, i.e. a curve with a
lower terminal policy rate. The ﬁrst is a material slowdown in the
growth outlook. The most extreme version of this is the 2008 policy
error by the European Central Bank – we all know what happened
after the ECB decided to hike in 2008. So far, the growth outlook

remains robust, and we do not see this as a likely path for 2027.
The second trigger of a more humped-shaped yield curve would be
a decline in inﬂation risks. Once inﬂation peaked in 2023, markets

Targeting Cookies

### Page 3

started repricing a lower neutral rate. In the near term, the curve still
held a hiking bias, but markets started pricing in a lower ECB rate
further out in the future. We believe this is the more likely path rates
will take and therefore expect a repricing lower by mid-2027.
Does Europe have a 'K-shaped' economy
like the US? And is there scope for
household savings to shield consumer
spending from higher energy prices in 2027?
Lower-income households generally suﬀer more from higher

energy prices than higher-income households, as they spend a
larger share of their income on energy and food. The energy shock
could therefore produce a more K-shaped consumption pattern.
However, Europe’s extensive social safety net provides important
protection for lower-income groups. Indeed, the consumer-
conﬁdence gap between the highest and lowest income quartiles
has not widened in 2026. Quite the opposite.
Since energy prices began rising, the savings rate has fallen,

cushioning the negative impact on consumption. However, with
households’ unemployment expectations increasing, it would be
overly optimistic to assume that the eurozone household savings
rate will continue to decline.
US growth has remained strong, but
deﬁcits are rising and a Democrat-run
Congress could put pressure on data centre
construction. Is the US heading towards a
debt crisis?
The US's ﬁscal position is poor. The government is currently
spending 4% of GDP on interest costs while running a primary

deﬁcit (tax revenue less departmental and programme spending) of
2.5% of GDP. The mid-term election results mean the primary deﬁcit
is pretty much locked in at 2.5% of GDP, with President Trump likely
vetoing any Democrat tax and spending policies approved in
Congress.

### Page 4

To keep the debt-to-GDP ratio stable, the economy needs to grow
faster than its debt pile to the tune of the primary deﬁcit. Today,
the US government is paying an average borrowing cost of 3.6%, so
nominal GDP needs to grow 6.1% per year to keep debt-to-GDP at
100% (6.1%-3.6%=2.5%). Amid rising government borrowing costs
and with nominal GDP growth closer to 5%, the debt-to-GDP ratio
looks set to climb.
But nothing is set in stone. For example, if AI leads to a

technological and productivity revolution that boosts growth and
tax revenue while lowering inﬂation and allowing interest costs to
fall, then the maths – and the outlook for the debt-to-GDP ratio –
changes dramatically.
Would the ECB lowering government
borrowing costs through bond purchases
encourage governments to borrow more,
worsening already-high debt levels? And is
it too soon for the ECB to ease policy, with
tools like QE?
Indeed, markets are inserting more disciplinary pressures on

governments that the European ﬁscal framework has failed to do.
This is why the ECB will be hesitant to restart bond purchases, at
least as long as governments are not subscribing to austerity
measures.
However, if the French ﬁscal crisis leads to contagion across the
eurozone while the rest of the eurozone sticks to the ﬁscal rules, the
ECB is likely to start its Transmission Protection Instrument to ring-
fence France and prevent contagion. The risk of starting any kind of

QE too early is indeed that it could create the wrong incentives for
ﬁscal policy.
The last US jobs report saw a small
uptick in wage growth. How likely is that to
accelerate, given tighter immigration policy
Your privacy is important
At ING we respect your right to choose how your data is used.
You can review and change your preferences at any time in
our Cookie policy.

Performance Cookies
Targeting Cookies

### Page 5

and labour shortages in some sectors, and
recent strength in the jobs data?
The best leading indicator for wage growth is the quits rate – the
proportion of workers quitting their jobs to move to a new position.
It peaked at 3% in 2022 as the post-Covid reopening fuelled intense
competition for workers. Companies responded by raising pay to
attract and retain staﬀ, pushing wage growth to 5.5%.
That situation has since reversed, with the quits rate down to 2%

and wage growth slowing sharply to just 3%. We are in a low-hire,
low-ﬁre economy with a lack of turnover in positions – few
companies are hiring, and few people are quitting, meaning
companies don't feel any real need to oﬀer big pay awards.
Some sectors are reporting shortages of skilled workers and there
will be pockets of strength, but until the quits rate moves
meaningfully higher, wage growth nationally will remain benign.
How will your outlook for the Federal

Reserve affect gold prices in 2027?
We expect the Fed to remain relatively cautious on rate cuts for
much of 2027, meaning monetary policy is unlikely to provide a
strong tailwind for gold early in the year. However, as expectations
for further easing build, lower yields and a potentially softer dollar
should become more supportive for bullion.
Combined with continued central bank buying, geopolitical
uncertainty and investor demand, we expect gold prices to be

supported in 2027, although periods of higher yields and dollar
strength could still trigger volatility.

根據機構LSEG統計，受到AI晶片、記憶體、硬碟等

龍頭台積電3Q26法說表示，受益於強勁AI需求與2
奈米開始貢獻營收，公司3Q26營收與獲利均優於市
場預期。3Q26高效運算與智慧手機營收比重分別達
68%、21%。高效運算營收年增高達53%、智慧手
機年減10%。AI代理與開源模型普及帶動先進製程

用量， 台 積 電 預 估 4Q26 美 元 營 收 將 年 增 62% ，
2026年美元營收預估達44%。由於AI晶片成長高於
60%，預估台積電2027年營收與獲利仍將續創新高。

重點摘要
• 雲端巨頭資本支出持平，伺
服器仍優於消費終端
《雲端巨頭資本支出持平，伺服器仍優於消費終端》
3Q26財報顯示，谷哥、亞馬遜、微軟與Meta在
2026年資本支出目標合計達7,300億美元，較上季
略減25億美元(-0.3%)，減少主要是微軟外部第三方
算力產能延遲導致資本支出目標下修50億美元，與
Meta上調25億美元相互抵消。雖然雲端巨頭支出
持平但資料中心晶片需求仍強勁。英特爾3Q26財報
顯示，受益於資料中心與AI事業帶動營收達165億
美元、年增21%。其中資料中心與人工智慧相關營
收73億美元續創歷史新高、年增78%，2027年相關
晶片仍將供不應求。反觀個人電腦營收81億美元、
年減5%。英特爾預估2026年個人電腦出貨量將年

減12%、2027年受到記憶體與儲存供給影響預估將
續減10%。
受到上半年消費者預期終端售價上漲提前換機，
3Q26個人電腦與智慧手機出貨均呈現大幅下滑。根
據IDC統計，3Q26全球個人電腦出貨量達6,270萬
台，較2025年同期衰退20%。同樣受到記憶體漲價
與供應短缺衝擊的智慧手機3Q26出貨量2.6億台，
較2025年同期衰退22%。由於未來記憶體與儲存價
格小幅調漲以及供給受限下，消費終端價格仍將維
持高價位。因此，IDC預估個人電腦與智慧手機
2027年將高機率維持衰退，預估要2028年出貨量
才有望復甦。

重點摘要
3
《類比晶片終端需求上升，工業、資料中心占比較
高供應商營收更具成長性》
德州儀器(TXN)3Q26營收60.6億美元、EPS 2.62美
元雙雙優於預期，4Q26財測營收58-63億美元，中
位數較市場普遍預期高出6%，反映資料中心市場
需求持續成長，使營收預測優於往年季節性回落。
以3Q26終端市場來看，工業營收年增超過40%、
季增約10%，成長來自航太國防、能源基礎設施等
領域；車用營收年增約15%、季增約10%，受益於
客戶庫存水準降低及電動車占比提高；資料中心營
收則年增約95%、季增20%，為成長最快的終端市
場。在營運方面，產能利用率提高以及2027年潛在
的價格調漲可能進一步擴大獲利成長空間，儘管資

本支出可能高於2026年的20~30億美元，但顯示終
端需求正擴大成長。
恩智浦(NXPI)3Q26營收37.9億美元、EPS 4.20美元
優於預期，但4Q26財測營收中位數38.5億美元略
低於市場預期。以3Q26終端市場來看，車用營收
20.7億美元、年增13%，成長主要來自軟體定義汽
車(SDV)產品貢獻；工業與物聯網營收8.02億美元、
年增39%，主要由資料中心、工廠自動化和醫療保
健等產業帶動；通訊基礎設施營收5.03億美元、年
增54%，得益於資料中心業務成長。恩智浦財報結
果顯示各終端市場需求持續上升，惟成長速度較慢
的車用營收占比超過五成，未來成長速度可能不及
工業和資料中心營收占比更高的同業。

4
《券商 CIO 調查顯示軟體支出預期持續增溫》
券商 CIO 調查指出 2027 年的企業 IT 採購預算仍維
持成長。CIO 現在預計 2026 年的 IT 採購預算將年
增 4.0%，自 2Q26 走高 20bps，連續 3 次增溫。
對於 2027 年 IT 採購預算的首次估計，CIO 預期年
增 4.0%，雖僅與更新後的 2026 年預期相符，但實
則是近 4 年來的開年新高。在這之中，軟體預算成
長優於領先硬體、網通與服務，預期年增 4.4%，且

自 2026 年加速 10bps。2027 年軟體支出的預期加
速可能進一步說明短期內 AI 並未帶來全面性衝擊。
資料來源：摩根史丹利，2026/10/12，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/20
人工智慧與機器學習 (AI/ML) 的支出優先性仍穩居
CIO 調查的第一名，佔比雖較前幾期調研回落，但
這應該是因為 CIO 對於開始增加其他項目的優先性。
AI 相關專案的預算仍最不可能被刪除，且淨占比
(即最不可能被刪除 – 最可能被刪除的比例)，3Q26
較 2Q26 大幅走揚 13ppts 至 17%。網路安全的支
出優先性則連 4 季走揚，且最不可能被刪除的淨占
比也走揚 1ppt 至 16%。持續反映出 AI 帶動的攻擊
面擴大、攻擊手法更加多元，與攻擊成本大幅走低，
導致企業在 AI 顯著產生正面效益前，優先選擇防範
未然的心態。而隨著 AI 的能力仍在快速發展，如近
期 Anthropic 研究即指出 AI 已可自我迭代，且小
型模型未來可能具備在物聯網裝置上自我複製的能

力，本行預期網安的需求可望仍持續強健。數位轉
型雖仍處第 3，但在本次券商調查中大幅地低於過
硬體 軟體 通訊設備 IT服務

Document 509: マレーシアGDP（2026年7-9月期）～5％台半ばの成長維持、AI関連輸出と民間投資が下支え

Source PDF: all report pdf/マレーシアGDP（2026年7-9月期）～5％台半ばの成長維持、AI関連輸出と民間投資が下支え.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-11-18
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

【結果概要】 
マレーシアの 2026年 7-9月期の実質 GDP成長率（速報値）1は前年同期比 5.5％となり、前期
（2026年4-6月期：同 6.0％）から減速した（図表１）。もっとも、統計局が 10月に公表した先行
推計値（同5.3％）から、引き続き高めの成長を維持した。 
需要面では、家計消費と政府消費の伸びが鈍化した一方、民間投資が機械・設備や建設関連を中
心に加速し、総固定資本形成は前期から伸びを高めた。外需では、世界的な AI関連需要の拡大を
背景に電気・電子（E&E）製品などの輸出が大幅に加速したものの、輸入も高い伸びを示したことか
ら、純輸出による成長率の押し上げは前期から縮小した。 
総じて、7-9月期は消費の減速や石油・天然ガスの生産減少が成長率を押し下げたものの、AI関
連需要を背景とする製造業の好調や民間投資の拡大に支えられ、5％台半ばの堅調な成長を維持し
た。

【支出別の詳細】 
家計最終消費支出は前年同期比 4.3％となり、前期の同 4.8％から伸びが鈍化した（図表２）。良
好な雇用環境や所得の増加、政府の家計支援策を背景に底堅く推移し、レストラン・ホテル（同
10.4％）や輸送（同 7.0％）を中心に消費が拡大した。もっとも、前年同期に実施された現金給付
の増額による反動もあり、消費全体の伸びは鈍化した。 
政府最終消費支出は前年同期比 1.2％となり、前期の同 7.6％から大幅に減速した。主に物品・
サービス関連支出の減少が伸びを抑えた。

1 2026年11月 18日、マレーシア統計局(DOSM)が 2026年7-9月期 GDPの速報値を公表した。

### Page 2

【産業別の詳細】 
サービス業は前年同期比 5.8％となり、前期の同 5.9％から小幅に減速したものの、引き続き成
長の主なけん引役となった（図表３）。情報・通信（同 8.7％）や金融・保険（同 5.1％）が前期か
ら伸びを高めた。特に情報・通信は、データセンター関連事業の拡大などを背景に高い伸びを維持
した。一方、卸・小売（同 4.3％）や運輸・倉庫（同 6.4％）、宿泊（同8.6％）、ビジネスサービス
（同 5.2％）は伸びが鈍化した。 
製造業は前年同期比 7.7％となり、前期の同 7.3％から加速した。主力の電気・電子・光学製品
が同 16.5％と前期の同14.4％から伸びを高め、世界的なAI関連需要の拡大を背景とする輸出向け
生産が全体をけん引した。また、非金属鉱物・金属製品は同 6.1％と前期から加速した。一方、石
油・化学・ゴム・プラスチック製品は同2.3％と前期から伸びが鈍化した。 
建設業は前年同期比 5.9％となり、前期の同 6.5％から減速した。非住宅建築が同 16.7％と前期
の同 9.6％から大幅に加速し、データセンター関連などの建設需要が引き続き全体を下支えした。
一方、専門建設活動は同6.6％と前期から減速したほか、住宅建築は同 0.2％、土木は同0.5％と低
い伸びにとどまった。 
農林水産業は前年同期比 1.4％となり、前期の同▲3.6％からプラスに転じた。主力のパーム油が
同 2.3％と前期の同▲9.5％から増加に転じたことが主因であり、各地での収量改善が生産回復に

### Page 3

【景気の見方と今後の注目点】 
7-9月期の実質 GDP成長率は前年同期比 5.5％と前期から減速したものの、1-9月期は同 5.6％となり、
政府が 10月に示した 2026年の成長率見通し（4.8～5.3％）の上限を上回るペースで推移している。中銀
も通年の成長率が 5％を上回ると見込んでおり、景気は引き続き堅調である。もっとも、家計消費や政府消
費の伸びが鈍化する一方、好調な輸出と民間投資が景気を支える構図となっている。 
先行きを展望する上では、AI 関連需要に支えられた輸出拡大と国内投資の持続性が焦点となる。世界
的な AI関連需要の拡大に伴い電気・電子（E&E）製品の輸出が好調なほか、データセンターや半導体分
野を中心に投資案件も進展している。こうした投資が建設・設備投資にとどまらず、関連産業や雇用の拡
大につながれば、成長の裾野が広がると期待される。ただし、輸出と投資の拡大に伴い中間財や資本財の
輸入も増加しており、国内付加価値の拡大につながるかを見極める必要がある。また、世界的な AI投資が
期待された収益を生まなければ、関連需要が鈍化するリスクもある。 
一方、内需面では、良好な雇用・所得環境に加え、2027年予算に盛り込まれた現金給付の拡充や所得
税減税、最低賃金の引き上げなどが 2027年の消費を下支えする見通しだ。また、承認済み投資案件の実
行が投資拡大を支えるとみられる。ただし、中東情勢の長期化に伴う供給制約やコスト上昇、石油・天然ガ
ス生産の回復の遅れは下振れリスクとなる。中銀は 2027 年の成長率を 4.2～5.2％と見込んでいるが、AI
関連需要の動向と資源生産の回復が成長の持続性を左右すると考えられる。

本資料記載のデータは各種の情報源から入手・加工したものであり、その正確性と完全性を保証するものではありません。 
また、本資料は情報提供が目的であり、記載の意見や予測は、いかなる契約の締結や解約を勧誘するものではありません。

事件  1
11/17 輝達第三季營收 1,158 億美元、年增 103% ，第四季指引 1,300 億美元
五⼤重點
1   輝達 11/17 公布第三季（截⾄ 10/25 ）營收 1,158 億美元，季增 20% 、年增 103% ，⾼於市場預期
約 1,088 億美元；資料中⼼營收 1,076 億美元，⾮ GAAP （⾮⼀般公認會計原則）每股盈餘 2.68 美

元。
2   第四季營收指引 1,300 億美元（正負 3% ，含多⼀週），⽑利率指引由第三季 74.9% 降⾄ 71% ⾄
72% ；公司指引未計⼊任何中國資料中⼼運算營收，第三季對中國 Hopper 出貨為 24 億美元。

### Page 2

3   財務⻑說明，第三季⽑利率⼩降反映 Vera Rubin 初期放量；存貨由 316 億升⾄ 397 億美元、應收
帳款天數由 60 天升⾄ 64 天，公司歸因於 Rubin 出貨節奏與部分投資級⼤客戶延⻑付款條件。
4   輝達正常盤收跌 0.44% ⾄ 155.46 美元，盤後漲約 3.8% ⾄ 161.35 美元；暫代執⾏⻑克雷斯稱
Rubin 「爆發性起步」，董事會主席表⽰無意讓⿈仁勳回任，將考慮內外部⼈選。
5   供應與產能承諾由 2,790 億增⾄ 3,900 億美元，主要為多年期記憶體採購，未來承諾總額 5,180
億美元；同⽇放空機構渾⽔（ Muddy Waters ）宣布放空輝達，治理疑慮未除。
三⼤結論
1   財報確認既有 AI 需求仍在加速交付，營收超預期與 Rubin 量產回應了換帥後對產品節奏的疑
慮；晶圓代⼯、先進封裝與記憶體的訂單能⾒度獲⽀撐，但⽑利指引下滑代表新平台爬坡成本由
輝達先吸收。
2   存貨、應收天數與採購承諾同步上升，且部分⼤客戶付款條件延⻑，顯⽰營收擴張伴隨營運資

⾦占⽤增加；這與⾼槓桿新雲端依賴外部融資的⾵險相連，供應鏈評價仍需以回款與現⾦流驗
證。
3   當輝達後續季度應收天數回落、存貨隨 Rubin 出貨下降，且台積電營收維持指引時，應分批維
持晶圓代⼯、先進封裝與記憶體核⼼部位；若新管理層縮減對新雲端的擔保或回款再延⻑，應先
降低 ODM 與⾼波動雲端衛星。
事件  2
11/17 紐約 Fed 總裁威廉斯稱通膨頑固，仍需升息但無須⼤動作
五⼤重點
1   紐約 Fed 總裁威廉斯 11/17 在紐約 Axios 論壇表⽰，通膨「頑固偏⾼」，關稅與能源衝擊正擴散⾄
更廣泛的通膨數據， Fed 要讓通膨回到 2% 仍有更多⼯作要做。
2   他認為經濟強勁且持續轉強、勞動市場穩健，「需求明顯跑贏供給」，但當前情勢不需要採取激
進的政策⾏動；⽬前聯邦資⾦利率⽬標區間為 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% 。
3   威廉斯為 FOMC （聯邦公開市場委員會）常任票委，談話後利率期貨隱含 12 ⽉升息⼀碼機率約
96% 、升息兩碼約 4% ，⼤幅升息的可能幾乎被排除。

4   他同時指出 AI 產業融資「變得更不透明且複雜」，但⽬前未看到 AI 帶來⾦融穩定⾵險；美國 10
年期公債殖利率 11/17 收約 5.10% ，仍處 5% 上⽅。

### Page 3

5   同⽇聖路易 Fed 總裁穆薩萊姆表⽰，實際政策利率需要再上升約 1 個百分點，貨幣政策必須⽴即
反應，鷹派官員主張的緊縮幅度明顯⼤於威廉斯。
三⼤結論
1   常任票委確認 12 ⽉升息⼀碼是基準、但排除兩碼，政策討論由「是否升息」轉為「還要升幾
次」；短端利率將停留⾼檔，⻑天期成⻑股的折現率壓⼒延續。
2   官員對緊縮幅度分歧，核⼼服務與能源擴散的數據將決定終點利率；短天期國庫券兼具收益與
再配置彈性，⻑天期公債在通膨與供給壓⼒未改善前仍應有限度配置。
3   當 11/19 公布的會議紀要與後續核⼼通膨顯⽰多數委員⽀持連續升息時，應維持短券核⼼並延
後增加⻑債久期；若核⼼服務⽉增回落且公債標售需求改善，才逐步延⻑存續期間。
事件  3
11/17 美國 10 ⽉零售銷售⽉增 0.2% ，⼯業⽣產⽉減 0.4%
五⼤重點
1   美國商務部普查局公布 10 ⽉零售銷售初值 7,482 億美元，⽉增 0.2% ，符合預期、低於 9 ⽉修正
後的 0.8% ；排除汽⾞及零件⽉增 0.6% ，整體年增 6.8% 。

2  Fed 公布 10 ⽉⼯業⽣產⽉減 0.4% ，遜於預期的⽉增 0.1% ，主因公⽤事業產出⽉減 4.3% 、礦業
減 0.8% ；製造業產出反⽽⽉增 0.3% ，產能利⽤率由 76.8% 降⾄ 76.4% 。
3   普查局說明，零售⽉增 0.2% 的信賴區間為正負 0.4% ，統計上無法確認為正成⻑；排除汽⾞後
⽉增 0.6% ⾼於整體，代表汽⾞及零件銷售下滑（推算）。
4   勞⼯統計局同⽇公布 10 ⽉進⼝物價⽉增 1.1% ，⾼於預期的 0.7% ，進⼝成本持續上升，與威廉
斯所說關稅與能源衝擊正向更廣泛物價擴散的判斷⼀致。
5   零售年增 6.8% 含物價上漲因素；商務部先前公布 9 ⽉個⼈儲蓄率已降⾄ 3.8% ，家庭以降低儲蓄
維持⽀出，⾯對油價或就業衝擊的緩衝變薄。
三⼤結論
1  10 ⽉消費放緩但未轉弱，⼯業⽣產下滑主要來⾃公⽤事業⽽⾮製造業，整體仍符合景氣擴
張；需求韌性加上進⼝物價⾛⾼，⽀持 Fed 在 12 ⽉續升息的基準。
2   名⽬零售成⻑含價格因素、家庭儲蓄緩衝下降，消費類股應區分客群收⼊穩定的企業與依賴信

⽤擴張的商品零售，不宜以名⽬銷售成⻑直接推估利潤。

### Page 4

3   當 11 ⽉零售、實質消費與初領失業⾦⼈數同時轉弱時，應提⾼現⾦與⾼品質債券⽐重；若消
費維持⽽核⼼通膨仍⾼，股票與⻑債可能同步承壓，應維持短券與現⾦流較強的股票。
事件  4
11/17 美軍空襲伊朗格什姆島⾶彈基地，布蘭特原油收 93.14 美元
五⼤重點
1   美國軍⽅官員表⽰，美軍戰機 11/17 以精準彈藥空襲伊朗⾰命衛隊位於荷莫茲海峽格什姆島的
⾶彈發射場，稱是回應伊朗針對美⽅⼈員與設施的攻擊，屬⾃衛⾏動。
2   伊朗地⽅官員稱攻擊未造成住宅與基礎設施損害；⾰命衛隊表⽰以無⼈機攻擊巴林伊薩空軍基
地的美軍雷達，海軍並稱第四艘油輪在荷莫茲海峽東部觸雷。
3   布蘭特原油 11/17 收 93.14 美元、漲 0.76% ，⻄德州原油收 86.01 美元、漲 0.34% ；盤中卡達外交
部稱美伊調解仍在持續，油價⼀度轉跌，漲幅受限。
4   伊朗總統裴澤斯基安表⽰，伊朗正以外交途徑尋求結束「不戰不和」局⾯，外⻑並已向阿曼蘇
丹通報結束戰爭的倡議，軍事衝突與和談訊號同時存在。

5   美軍先前稱已清除荷莫茲主要航道⽔雷，第四艘油輪觸雷顯⽰航道⾵險未消除；該海峽平時承
載全球約五分之⼀⽯油運量，美國仍對伊朗港⼝實施海上封鎖。
三⼤結論
1   美⽅由 11/16 暫緩打擊轉為 11/17 有限度空襲，地緣溢價重回油價，但調解仍在進⾏、布蘭特僅
⼩漲，市場仍把衝突定價為有限升級⽽⾮全⾯斷供。
2   油價停在 90 美元附近，能源通膨向核⼼物價擴散的⾵險仍在，與 Fed 官員對能源衝擊的擔憂⼀
致；原油與能源股宜維持⼩型事件部位，不以單⼀軍事新聞擴⼤配置。
3   當荷莫茲觸雷與攻擊擴⼤、布蘭特重回 100 美元以上時，應提⾼能源衛星作為通膨避險；若美
伊和談取得進展且供給修復，應降低原油部位並留意上游獲利下修。
事件  5
11/17 台股再跌 947 點收 40,092 點，外資連 2 ⽇賣超
五⼤重點

### Page 5

1   台灣加權指數 11/17 收 40,092.11 點，下跌 947.97 點、跌幅 2.31% ，盤中最低 39,786.45 點；櫃買
指數跌 3.92% ，上市櫃下跌 1,767 家、上漲僅 454 家。
2   台積電收 2,170 元、跌 2.03% ；上市三⼤法⼈合計賣超 547.2 億元，其中外資賣超 380.9 億元、
⾃營商賣超 157.0 億元，外資近 20 ⽇累計賣超約 1.14 兆元。
3   ⾼盛 11/17 將台股評等由「加碼」降⾄「中⽴」，加權指數⽬標由 57,500 點降⾄ 50,000 點；台股
早盤⼀度跌 3.1% 後短暫翻紅，終場仍收低。
4   兩⽇累計下跌約 3,583 點、跌幅約 8.2% （推算）；賣壓延續輝達換帥後的治理疑慮與美國遠端
算⼒管制，美股晶⽚股在輝達財報前同樣遭到賣壓。
5   上市融資餘額近 20 ⽇減少約 83 萬張，仍在近⼀年中段；台股市值加權本益⽐約 18.9 倍、預估本
益⽐ 17.5 倍，評價已隨指數回落，但外資賣壓尚未停歇。

三⼤結論
1   兩⽇重挫主要反映 AI ⿓頭治理與出⼝管制帶來的評價修正；輝達隨後公布的營收與 Rubin 出貨
優於預期，台灣上游交付基本⾯未⾒轉弱，股價與基本⾯的落差擴⼤。
2   外資持續賣超、企業保留美元與美台利差擴⼤，台幣承壓格局未變；台股⾼評分與台幣低評分
可同時成⽴，持有台股的本幣報酬須另計匯率影響。
3   當 11/20 公布的 10 ⽉外銷訂單延續強勁、外資賣超收斂時，應分批維持晶圓代⼯與先進封裝核
⼼部位；若融資再擴張或客戶回款轉弱，應先降低 ODM 與⾼波動中游衛星。

11/17 Anthropic 獲准在那斯達克掛牌，維持每股 70 ⾄ 78 美元發⾏區間
五⼤重點
1  Anthropic 11/17 向美國證管會提交修正版 S-1 ，發⾏規模與條件不變，每股價格區間維持 70 ⾄
78 美元，並獲准以代號 ANT 在那斯達克全球精選市場掛牌，預計 11/19 開始交易。
2   本次發⾏ 8.0135 億股新股，創辦⼈另出售 2,365 萬股；以區間中值 74 美元計，新股募資約 593

億美元（推算），另有 260 億美元⾮投票權 N 類股私募同步進⾏。
3   發⾏完成後亞⾺遜將持有 14.7% 流通股本、 33.9% N 類股，若其可轉債全數轉股將升⾄
23.8% 、 48.2% ；亞⾺遜與 Google 兩⼤客戶合計持股逾 28% 。

### Page 6

4   招股⽂件顯⽰， 2026 年 1 ⾄ 9 ⽉營收 332.4 億美元，第三季 169.6 億美元， 10 ⽉底年化營收約 810
億美元；同期營業虧損 12.6 億美元，帳⾯淨損多數為⾮現⾦評價調整。
5   以價格區間計，隱含市值約 9,175 億⾄ 1.02 兆美元； Altimeter 執⾏⻑表⽰已申請 50 億美元配售
額度，富時羅素通知最快可於 12/11 納⼊羅素 1000 指數。
三⼤結論
1  AI 模型業者⾸度以公開市場定價，⽤量成⻑能否抵銷 Token （計費單位）降價，將直接影響市
場對 AI 終端變現的信⼼，並回頭牽動上游算⼒需求的評價。
2   年化營收不等於已實現收⼊，帳⾯虧損也含⼤量⾮現⾦項⽬，評價須拆解使⽤量、單位收費、
算⼒成本與股權稀釋；⼤客戶交叉持股使營收與雲端資本⽀出⾼度連動。
3   當掛牌後付費留存與⽑利改善、算⼒⽀出占營收⽐續降時，應以正向看待 AI 軟體與上游算⼒需
求；若 Token 價格跌幅快於⽤量成⻑或上市後估值劇烈修正，應降低對模型與軟體⾼估值部位的

容忍度。
事件  7
11/16 Fed 主席華許提出 AI 三種經濟情境，納⼊脆弱性評估
五⼤重點
1  Fed 主席華許 11/16 為舊⾦⼭ Fed 活動準備的講稿表⽰， Fed 幕僚已開始推演 AI 加速普及對美國
經濟的不同情境，並強調這些情境不是預測。
2   第⼀種情境是 AI 推升⽣產⼒與成⻑、由雇主與勞⼯共享繁榮，第⼆種是經濟活動增加的好處被
⼤規模失業抵銷；第三種是技術不如預期，引發股價下跌與經濟放緩。
3   華許稱 Fed 的⾓⾊不是⽀持或反對 AI ，⽽是評估 AI 可能帶來的潛在脆弱性，包括遭不良⾏為者
利⽤造成擾亂，以及對勞動市場的重⼤衝擊。
4  Fed 主席把「 AI 不如預期導致股市下跌」列為正式情境，代表 AI 投資回落對⾦融市場與景氣的
衝擊，已被納⼊央⾏的政策評估範圍。
5   講稿未對 12 ⽉利率決策表態； Fed 10 ⽉以 10 ⽐ 2 維持利率 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% ，⽬前利率期貨已幾
乎完全定價 12 ⽉升息⼀碼，短期政策仍由通膨數據主導。

三⼤結論

### Page 7

1  Fed 把 AI 泡沫破裂列為情境，意味 AI 資本⽀出若未能變現，貨幣政策可能同時⾯對股價修正與
景氣放緩； AI 相關資產的評價不能只看需求成⻑，也要看現⾦回收。
2   ⼤規模失業情境將改變 Fed 對就業⽬標的判斷，⽣產⼒情境則可能降低通膨壓⼒；這是⻑期框
架，短期利率仍以核⼼通膨與就業數據為依據。
3   當 AI 企業⾃由現⾦流持續為負、融資成本上升且變現覆蓋度下滑時，應降低⾼槓桿雲端與⾼估
值 AI 衛星；若雲端營收持續加速並帶動現⾦流改善，才提⾼ AI 供應鏈的⾵險權重。
事件  8
11/17 歐盟執委會秋季預測：法國今年⾚字 5.5% ，明年成⻑下修⾄ 0.6%
五⼤重點
1   歐盟執委會 11/17 公布秋季經濟預測，歐元區 2026 與 2027 年 GDP 均成⻑ 1.2% 、 2028 年 1.3% ；
2026 年通膨預估 3.2% ， 2027 年 3.1% ， 2028 年才回落⾄ 2.1% 。
2   法國 2026 年財政⾚字預估擴⼤⾄ GDP 的 5.5% ，⾼於前次預測 5.1% 與政府⽬標 5.4% ； 2027 年

僅⼩降⾄ 5.4% ，仍遠⾼於 5% ⽬標， 2027 年政府債務由 120.2% 上修⾄ 122.1% 。
3   執委會說明，法國經濟成⻑低迷與公債利息⽀出增加，將抵銷 2027 年預算中的⼤規模財政整
頓；法國 2027 年成⻑預測由 1.1% ⼤幅下修⾄ 0.6% 。
4   德國 2027 年成⻑預測由 0.9% 上修⾄ 1.0% 、⻄班⽛由 1.9% 升⾄ 2.1% 、義⼤利由 0.6% 升⾄
0.7% ；歐元區整體⾚字將由 2025 年 3.0% 升⾄ 2026 年 3.5% 。
5   法國 CAC 40 指數 11/17 收跌 1.62% ⾄ 8,216.78 點；同⽇法國爆發新⼀輪⽰威，內政部統計全國
約 14.6 萬⼈參與，政治與財政壓⼒仍未緩解。
三⼤結論
1   歐元區通膨預估到 2027 年仍⾼於 3% ，⽀持 ECB 維持緩步升息路徑；成⻑僅約 1.2% ，政策緊縮
與能源成本將持續壓抑企業獲利，景氣擴張但⼒道有限。
2   法國⾚字與債務上修、成⻑下修，是區域中最脆弱的⼀環；德國與南歐預測反⽽上修，主權⾵

險未全⾯擴散，不宜把法國壓⼒外推為全歐危機。
3   當法國預算審議再陷僵局、法德利差擴⼤時，應降低法國內需與主權債曝險；歐洲股市維持中
性偏審慎，偏好低負債、具海外收⼊與成本轉嫁能⼒的公司，待 PMI 廣度與能源改善再增加週期
曝險。

事件  9
11/17 美銀經理⼈調查：科技股淨超配⽐例降⾄ 12% ，創 2006 年來最⼤降幅
五⼤重點
1   美國銀⾏ 11/17 公布 11 ⽉全球基⾦經理⼈調查（ 11/6 ⾄ 11/12 進⾏），科技股淨超配⽐例由 10 ⽉
的 37% 降⾄ 12% ，為 2006 年 6 ⽉以來最⼤降幅。
2   創紀錄的淨 38% 受訪者認為企業過度投資 AI ， 52% 預期未來六個⽉將有⼤型雲端業者宣布削減
資本⽀出；淨 32% 預期全球 CPI （消費者物價指數）未來 12 個⽉下降。
3   美銀策略師哈特尼特表⽰，市場對 AI 繁榮的狂熱已經落幕，投資⼈強烈呼籲企業暫緩 AI 相關投
⼊。
4   調查期間涵蓋 11/12 輝達換帥前後；經理⼈⼤舉減碼科技，與輝達 11/17 公布營收年增 103% 、
Rubin 量產的基本⾯形成明顯落差。
5   費城半導體指數 11/17 盤中⼀度跌逾 3% ，終場收漲 0.34% ⾄ 9,642.88 點，仍較 52 週⾼點 14,655

點低約 34% （推算），科技股已⼤幅修正。
三⼤結論
1   經理⼈⼤幅減碼科技反映對 AI 資本⽀出回報的疑慮，⽽⾮已確認的需求下滑；上游財報與出貨
仍強，情緒與基本⾯的落差可能放⼤科技股的雙向波動。
2   若⼤型雲端業者真的削減資本⽀出，衝擊將先落在依賴外部融資的新雲端與中游組裝；具定價
權與現⾦流的晶圓代⼯、記憶體⿓頭受影響相對較⼩。
3   當雲端業者下⼀季財報確認資本⽀出維持、且科技持股已降⾄低檔時，可分批增加⾼品質上
游；若有⼤型雲端業者正式下修資本⽀出，應先降低 ODM 、⾼槓桿雲端與⾼估值軟體部位。
事件  10
11/17 京東第三季營收年增 3.5% 、淨利 65 億元，美股 ADR 收跌 7.66%
五⼤重點
1   京東 11/17 在港交所公告第三季營收 3,096 億元⼈⺠幣，年增 3.5% ；歸屬普通股股東淨利 65 億
元，⾼於去年同期 53 億元，淨利率由 1.8% 升⾄ 2.1% 。
2   ⾮美國公認會計準則淨利 83 億元，去年同期 58 億元，⾮ GAAP 淨利率由 1.9% 升⾄ 2.7% ，獲利

成⻑明顯快於營收。

### Page 9

3   京東港股 11/17 收跌 2.68% ⾄ 105.2 港元，美股 ADR 收跌 7.66% ⾄ 25.43 美元，接近 52 週低點
24.51 美元。
4   營收較去年同期僅增加約 105 億元⼈⺠幣（推算）；國家統計局 11/16 公布 10 ⽉社會消費品零售
年增僅 0.4% ，與平台營收低速成⻑相互印證。
5   淨利率雖升⾄ 2.1% ，仍屬低檔，獲利改善主要來⾃利潤率提升⽽⾮銷售規模擴張。
三⼤結論
1   京東獲利率提升但營收僅低個位數成⻑，反映中國居⺠消費與內需尚未修復；平台股獲利改善
不等於消費需求回溫，股價反應偏弱顯⽰市場更重視營收動能。
2   中國 10 ⽉零售與固定投資轉弱，政策尚未修復居⺠收⼊與資產價格；中國股市宜維持選擇性
衛星，優先財務健全的出⼝技術製造，內需平台不宜全⾯提⾼權重。
3   當中國零售、⺠間投資與居⺠收⼊連續改善、平台營收重回雙位數成⻑時，才考慮提⾼中國內
需股權重；若零售持續低於 1% 且價格競爭延續，應維持低配並以已知⽀出匹配⼈⺠幣部位。

待確認
1. 推算值：事件 3 「汽⾞及零件銷售下滑」由整體與排除汽⾞增幅推算；事件 5 兩⽇累計跌點與跌幅；事件 6 新股募資
約 593 億美元；事件 9 費半距 52 週⾼點跌幅；事件 10 營收增加額。
2. 單⼀來源或⼆⼿來源：事件 1 市場預期 1,088 億美元、盤後漲幅為媒體與報價資料；事件 2 利率期貨機率與 10 年期殖
利率 5.10% 為市場報價，威廉斯為論壇談話⾮官⽅講稿；事件 6 營收、虧損、隱含市值與私募⾦額取⾃招股⽂件的第
三⽅整理，尚未直接核對 SEC 原⽂；事件 7 華許講稿內容取⾃快訊，未取得 Fed 官網全⽂；事件 9 為美銀調查轉述。
3. 事件 6 Anthropic 預計 11/18 定價、 11/19 交易，最終價格與規模尚未確定；事件 1 管理層在法說會提及的年度成⻑⽬
標未取得逐字稿，未寫⼊。
4. 尚未公布：英國 10 ⽉ CPI （ 11/18 ）、 10 ⽉ FOMC 會議紀要（ 11/19 ）、台灣 10 ⽉外銷訂單（ 11/20 ）、川普訪華與

11/19 深圳三⽅會晤結果、德國內閣對 ECB ⼈事的決定。
5. 與上⼀輪⽐對（「全球⼗⼤財經事件｜ 2026/11/17 07:51 （台灣時間）」）：已列之台股 11/16 重挫、美國商務部遠端
算⼒管制、美股 11/16 科技股重挫、哈瑪克談話、紐約州製造業指數、布蘭特 11/16 下跌、中國 10 ⽉社零與⼯業增加
值、⽇本第三季 GDP 、中國證實三⽅會晤、英國 2 年期殖利率觸及 5% ，本輪均未再列為事件；事件 10 僅以⼀句背景
提及中國 10 ⽉社零。
6. 新事實晚於上⼀輪資料同步時間（ 11/17 07:51 ）：事件 4 美軍空襲格什姆島（台灣時間 11/17 23:55 起）、事件 5 台股
11/17 收盤（台灣時間 11/17 13:30 ），均以新事實為主體；事件 1 輝達財報與上⼀輪的股價事件屬不同公告。
7. 72 ⼩時內無主要央⾏實際調整利率或準備⾦率；⼈⾏外匯存款準備⾦率 11/15 ⽣效已於 11/16 07:51 報告列出。
8. 48 ⾄ 72 ⼩時⼊選 0 件： 11/15 ⾄ 11/16 上午的事件多已於上⼀輪列出，其餘份量低於較新事件。

9. 九⼤分類中，台灣經濟與央⾏近 72 ⼩時無重⼤官⽅數據或央⾏動作；⽇本經濟與央⾏僅有前⽇銀總裁⿊⽥與經濟
財政⼤⾂城內實的原則性談話，份量不⾜；歐洲、⽇本公司無⾜夠份量的財報或公告；中美國際政治經濟競爭⽅⾯，
川普訪華與深圳會晤尚未舉⾏，故未單獨成件。

### Page 10

10. 六⼤實質內涵皆有涵蓋：重要經濟數據（事件 3 、 8 ）、政府與財政政策（事件 8 ）、央⾏官員發⾔（事件 2 、 7 ）、劇
烈⾏情⽰警（事件 5 ）、⼤型公司財報（事件 1 、 10 ）、 AI 重要發展（事件 1 、 6 、 7 、 9 ）。

動態更新 PC 零組件 ‧ 台灣

技嘉 
(2376.TW/2376 TT)

2027 年伺服器營收展望強勁 
重要訊息 
3Q26 EPS 12.8 元，優於我們預估和市場共識，主因獲利率表現亮眼。管理
層表示，2027 年 AI 伺服器需求爆發，公司將持續擴充產能。 
評論及分析 
3Q26 EPS 優於預期。3Q26 EPS 年增 181%、季增 30%至 12.80 元，優於
我們預估 19%、市場共識 30%，因毛利率表現亮眼。毛利率季增 4.4ppts
至 14% ， 高 於我們預估 2.8ppts ， 因產品組合優化 ( 伺服器營收比重自 
2Q26 的 81-82%降至 77-78%)、存貨跌價損失迴轉 (影響毛利率 0.5ppts)，
以及提前備料使零組件成本降低。儘管費用率 5.7%偏高，營利率仍季增 
2.7ppts 至 8.3%，高於我們預估 1.1ppts。 
伺服器推升 4Q26 營收。伺服器帶動 10 月營收月增 6%至 670 億元，達我
們 4Q26 營收預估之 38%。4Q26 開始出貨 VR200 機櫃，NVL72 機櫃拉貨
將帶動 4Q26 伺服器營收季增幅加速。此外，管理層展望 4Q26 消費性營收
季增。儘管產品組合轉弱 (NVL72 機櫃佔伺服器營收比重自 3Q26 的 30%拉
升至 30%以上) 使 4Q26 毛利率季減，但因伺服器展望更強，上修 4Q26 營
收與 EPS 至 10.91 元，2026 年 EPS 上修至 41.43 元，年增 128%。 
2027 年 AI 伺服器需求強勁。管理層指出 AI 訓練&推論、代理式 AI 及儲存
等多元應用需求強勁，並展望 2027 年伺服器營收強勁年增。此外，Nebius 
(荷) 近期將 2026 年資本支出展望自 200-250 億美元上修至 270 億美元，並
展望 2027 年資本支出年增 57% (中位數) 至 400-450 億美元，將帶動技嘉
2027 年伺服器營收成長。技嘉透過雲端 AI 到邊緣 AI 的整體解決方案，承
接 更 多 主權 AI 與 企業 客 戶。 為 滿 足客 戶需 求 ，2027 年 資 本 支出 將年 增 
38%至 40 億元 (2026 年 28-30 億元)，用於美國及馬來西亞擴產。我們上
修 2027 年營收預估至 7,080 億元，年增 54% (營收比重 84%)，相較 2026
年 4,610 億元 (79%)。儘管調高費用率及利息費用預估，仍上修 2027 年
EPS 至 40.45 元，年減 2%。 
投資建議 
反映獲利成長減速，我們下修目標本益比以及目標價至 455 元，改以 12 倍
完全稀釋後 2027 年 EPS 預估為基準，評等維持「增加持股」。 
投資風險 
伺服器 GPU 供給吃緊；客戶資料中心建置遞延。 
增加持股‧維持

10%，而伺服器產品則佔 62%，其他產品佔 4%。

台北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號

巴西右翼有望重掌大權，把握拉美股市中長期機會

巴西政權或再次轉移，財政紀律與市場化改革預期升溫，加上拉美貨幣高息優
勢，股市在能源與金融產業帶動下維持強勢，建議投資人採定期定額方式布局拉
美，掌握中長期成長機會。 
出刊日：2026/10/16 
275 期

### Page 2

2026

02-2771-6699 
 www.fubon.com 
台北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號 
動見觀瞻

本期內容： 
1. 「巴西川普」之
子爆冷領先 
2. 預測市場押注博
索納羅勝選 
3. 拉美貨幣走勢強
勁 
4. 拉美股市前景光
明

重點摘要 
■ 巴西總統大選首輪由弗拉維奧·博索納
羅爆冷領先，反映高物價、治安與貪腐
疑慮持續侵蝕盧拉支持度，「反盧拉」
陣營選票將在二輪投票加速整合。

■ 今年拉美貨幣表現相對強勢，巴西里
拉與墨西哥披索受高利率及資金流入
支撐，其中巴西財政政策改善期待亦
有助降低貨幣風險溢酬。

■ MSCI 拉美指數今年累計上漲 23%，
能源股以 92%漲幅領軍，金融與內需
股同步走強；後續持續看好能源、金融
及巴西政策改革帶來的機會。

拉美股市在巴西首輪選舉公布後大漲

資料來源：Bloomberg/北富銀整理, 2026/10/7

巴西右翼有望重掌大權，把握拉美股市中長期機會

### Page 3

2026

02-2771-6699 
 www.fubon.com 
台北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號

▋ 「巴西川普」之子爆冷領先 
2026 年 10 月 5 日，巴西最高選舉法院公
布 2026 年總統選舉首輪投票結果，由於未
有候選人獲得超過 50%選票，將由得票率
最高的兩位候選人於 10 月 25 日進行二輪
投票。投票結果顯示，二輪投票將由前總統
雅伊爾·博索納羅之子、自由黨候選人弗拉
維奧·博索納羅和巴西現任總統、勞工黨候
選人盧拉角逐巴西總統大位。

圖一: 博索納羅在首輪選舉爆冷領先

資料來源：巴西最高選舉法院/北富銀整理, 2026/10/7

現任總統盧拉曾在 2003 年至 2011 年就任
巴西第 35 任總統，並在 2022 年復出並擊
敗有「巴西川普」之稱的前總統雅伊爾·博索
納羅，使 雅伊爾·博索納羅成為 1985 年後巴
西首位連任失敗的總統。過去 4 年，盧拉總
統任期期間，巴西經濟穩健成長、失業率也
持續下降，但食品與飲料等民生消費項目通
膨嚴重，加上治安惡化與貪腐議題重燃(勞
工黨長期負面印象)，讓「反盧拉」陣營持續
猛 攻 ，使 得 盧拉 的民 調支 持 度從 未 超過
40%，且在 2025 年 2 月之最低點僅有 24%。
儘管後續出現反彈，也只回升至 35%上下
(上任初期最高峰水準)。 
另一方面，自由黨候選人弗拉維奧·博索納
羅長期參與巴西國內政治活動，在 2003 至
2019 年就任里約熱內盧州議會議員，並在
2019 年後進一步前進聯邦政壇，就任巴西
聯邦參議院議員，整體政治年資超過 20 年。
此次參選，其主要政見為「財政緊縮＋減稅
與小政府＋強硬治安」，透過減稅、財政緊
縮及政府縮編等動作降低民眾經濟壓力並
減少政府貪腐可能，再預期透過強硬手段打
擊犯罪，全面針對盧拉執政期間的弱點提出
改善方案，使其成為「反盧拉」陣營共主。

▋ 預測市場押注博索納羅勝選 
Kalshi 數據顯示，預測市場早在首輪投票前
便逐步轉向弗拉維奧·博索納羅。一個月前，
儘管選前民調仍顯示盧拉領先，但博索納羅
勝選機率已升至 55%，顯示市場更著眼於
二輪選舉後的選票重分配效應。而首輪結果
爆冷更加強化其勝選預期，目前弗拉維奧·
博索納羅最終勝選機率已升至 85%，市場
顯然押注反盧拉與偏右翼選票將向博索納
羅集中，使巴西政權輪替可能性明顯升高。

圖二: 博索納羅勝選機率達 85%

資料來源：Kalshi, 2026/10/7

### Page 4

2026

02-2771-6699 
 www.fubon.com 
台北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號

▋ 拉美貨幣走勢強勁 
今年以來美元表現強勢，主要受惠中東地緣
政治帶來的避險需求及美債殖利率、升息預
期帶來的買盤支撐。然而，並非所有貨幣對
美元皆呈現弱勢，以拉美、新亞等區域的新
興市場貨幣兌美元表現最佳。墨西哥披索兌
美元年初以來升值約 2%，巴西里拉更升值
逾 5%，表現僅次於韓元與澳幣，顯示資金
對拉美高息貨幣仍具一定偏好。值得注意的
是，巴西里拉今年強勢亦與巴西維持高利率
環境有關，高額利差持續提供套利交易誘因。

圖三: 拉美貨幣兌美元表現強勢

資料來源：Goldman Sachs, 2026/9/28

市場近期開始交易巴西政權輪替可能，若博
索納羅陣營最終勝選，並落實財政緊縮、控
制政府債務及縮減公共支出等政策，有望降
低巴西財政風險溢酬，進一步強化里拉支撐。
相較之下，墨西哥披索升幅較為溫和，反映
拉美貨幣雖整體受惠高息優勢，但各國財政、
利率前景仍將決定後續表現。

▋ 拉美股市前景光明 
拉美股市今年延續強勢表現，截至 10 月 6
日 MSCI 拉美指數累計上漲 23%，其中能
源類股大漲 92%居冠，非必需消費、公用
事業及金融亦分別上漲 36%、33%及 28%。
能源股表現突出主要受惠油價走高，以及拉
美原油供給地位提升，巴西深海油田、阿根
廷 Vaca Muerta 頁岩油等產量持續擴張，
使區域能源企業同時受惠價格與產量成長

圖四: 拉美股市同樣以能源產業表現最佳

資料來源：Bloomberg/北富銀整理, 2026/10/7

展望後市，持續看好能源與金融兩大產業，
前者受惠拉美油氣產能擴張與全球能源供
應版圖轉移，後者則可望受惠高利率環境下
的利差優勢。若巴西大選最終出現政黨輪替，
市場預期將延續財政紀律、減稅及監管鬆綁
等題材交易，有望降低巴西資產風險溢酬，
成為拉美股市下一階段的重要催化劑。

▋ 結論 
巴西大選爆冷使政權可能再次轉移，財政紀
律與市場化改革預期升溫，加上拉美貨幣高
息優勢，股市在能源與金融產業股價帶動下
維持強勢。展望後市，政治改革與資源行情
仍具支撐，惟短期波動難免，建議投資人持
續採定期定額方式布局拉美市場，掌握中長
期成長機會。

市場展望投資策略
 投等債布局風險相對小：十年美債殖利率已逐步反應
FED升息預期，過去經驗，在此利差水準(十年殖利率與
政策利率利差)布局，風險承擔相對較小。然而，債務問
題依舊是短期利率可能上升的壓力。(詳見Page 13)
 持續買進台股：台股持續創下新高，台積電10/15召開
3Q26法說，高機率繼續釋放樂觀前景，推升後續上漲
動能，同步助益台股前景，4Q26台股有望繼續成為領
漲全球股市的關鍵。(詳見Page 17)
 產業布局鎖定大型科技股：AI趨勢明確，唯一的不確定
性源自利率風險是否再次攀升，衝擊股市估值。Mag7、
半導體巨頭成長性高且估值普遍相對便宜，相對其餘產
業類股更能抵禦利率衝擊。(詳見Page 15)
 匯市展望：升息預期疊加歐洲債務疑慮支撐美元強勢，
然而隨著美德利差短期飆升過快，先前漲勢兇猛的美元
或暫時歇息，維持高檔盤整。然而，法國政局不確定性
恐一路拖至2027年6月，中長期資金仍可能持續從歐洲
流入美國， 支撐美元長期走勢。 本週美元指數區間

100.5~103。
 債市展望：儘管法國、美國、日本政治官員相繼釋出試
圖穩定市場情緒的財政計畫或談話，但市場反應明顯不
買單，願景與口號終究無法安撫投資人信心，殖利率短
期 仍 有 續 攀 升 風 險 ， 本 週 美 債 十 年 殖 利 率 區 間 看 在
5.2%~5.4%。
 股市展望 : 3Q26財報季將至，市場預估S&P500整體
EPS年增率高達24.5%，強 勁 的 盈 餘成 長 依 舊是 支撐
4Q26股市創高關鍵，惟短期仍須留意利率攀升對於市
場廣度的衝擊。本週標普500指數區間7,650~7,950。

➢ 2010~2022年巴西四次總統大選後股市普遍走跌(90個
交易日) ，唯一上漲是2018年(雅伊爾.波索納羅勝選)，

西殖利率走低。目前巴西正經歷同樣的市況，市場預
期未來1年巴西央行仍有7碼降息空間。
➢ 目前MSCI巴西股市預期PE為9.4X，不僅低於20年平均
的10.4X，也低於過去四次總統大選前的水位，為後續
股市創造上漲空間。

### Page 7

通膨有望持穩，官員談話或淡化升息預期
7
重點提醒
➢ 本週全球總經重點聚焦在通膨數據，以及FED官員談話
態度，在美債殖利率持續飆升之際，成為市場短期定價
的風向球。
➢ 8月美國核心PCE維持3%的穩定增速，再次證明美國並
未見到通膨上升的壓力， 同時9 月即時通膨指標
Truflation平均年增率2.41%，也同樣低於8月的2.51%，
然而通膨僵固性確實存在。9月FED剛展開升息循環，利
率上升對於物價的壓抑效果尚未體現，預期9月CPI仍將
維持穩定增速。
➢ Hammack 9月談話明顯鷹派，認為企業與家庭或將高
物價視為常態，使高通膨更難消除，政策須維持限制性
立場。而Waller 9月談話相對鴿派，認為若通膨未明顯
攀升，仍傾向維持利率不變，在期貨市場預期未來FED
仍有4碼的升息空間情況下，或有機會降溫市場情緒。

### Page 9

法國財政見不到曙光，美元維持強勢預期
9
短期美德利差或引導美元暫歇 圖表解讀
➢ 法國財政疑慮引發歐元走弱，同時疊加美國升息、債
務持續性問題，美德利差快速飆升，推升美元指數走
強。然而，短期美德10年利差上升速度過快，加上殖
利率已完全反應FED升息預期，美元指數升勢或獲得暫
緩空間。
➢ 然而，中長期仍看好美元維持相對強勢。2027年4月
18號~5月2號法國將舉行總統大選，預期新任總統將
於6月再次解散國會改選以解決國會僵局。但以目前賭
盤機率，極右派Le Pen和中間派Philippe分別以33%
和26%位居前二，未有黨派能單獨過半的情勢仍高機
率延續至2027年。
➢ 在市場無法見到明確的政治僵局突破或財政赤字緩解
的跡象前，不確定因素將延續至2027年，持續干擾歐
元，引導美元強勢。

### Page 10

海外資金匯回潛在買盤提供日圓支撐
10
日本殖利率並未如美債、法債反應劇烈 圖表解讀
➢ 相對於「震央核心」的法國、美國，9月日本殖利率上
升幅度明顯較低，在日本債務/GDP比重高於法國、美
國的情況下，此舉並不尋常，關鍵在於市場期待規模
高達2兆美元的政府退休金投資基金(GPIF)有望降低海
外債券，提高國內債券持有比重。
➢ 10月日本十年公債標售倍數達3.76X，連續3個月回升，
也證明市場對日本財政擔憂並未在此次全球財政疑慮
中爆發，一定程度上緩解日圓貶勢。
➢ 此波日圓雖未隨美元強勢而大幅貶值，但美元/日圓短
期仍有向上回測160價位可能，市場將再次挑戰日本政
府對於阻貶日圓的決心，但在基金、保險公司等潛在
資金匯回的買盤下，預期貶破前波低點164附近的機率
不高。

11
債市表現說明
➢ 美國公債：上週美國十年公債拍賣投標倍數達2.77倍，創

為債市提供暫時性的喘息空間。

約5bp，但就目前水準值80bp而言，仍遠低於過去5年平
均的102bp，美國投等債企業財務體質依舊穩健。
➢ 非投等債：相較於投等債的穩定，上週全球非投等債的
信用利差上升幅度約40bp，水準值逐漸逼近今年3月美
伊戰爭爆發時的波段高峰，尤其CCC及以下評等更是主要
拖累關鍵，市場對於中小企業能否在利率攀升之際，維
持獲利穩定感到懷疑。

法國政局為財政壓力增添不確定性
12
法國CDS飆升至歐債危機以來新高
圖表解讀
➢ 10/1法國政府提出2027年預算草案，包含凍結公務員
薪資、凍結部分退休金、削減地方預算和健保支出等
緊 縮 政 策 ， 試 圖 將 年 度 財 政 赤 字 /GDP 比 重 控 制 在
5%(2026年為5.4%)。然而，分裂國會的政治僵局讓市
場預期預算案難以順利通過，高盛也下調2026、2027
年法國赤字率將達-5.3%。
➢ 近年法國政局的動盪便是預算案無法順利達成共識的
代價，2027年4月18~5月2號法國將舉行總統大選，
目前民調第一的極右派Le Pen更是讓市場存在不確定
性，同時6月又可能面臨國會重新選舉，財政擔憂將持
續干擾法國殖利率走勢。
➢ 儘管美國同樣面臨財政壓力，但美元霸權，加上美國
以本國貨幣發行債務，實際要出現違約的機率極低，
也是9月低點至今(截至10/2)，美國CDS僅上升約9bp，
然而法國CDS卻已上升超過40bp，創下歐債危機以來
新高。資料來源： Bloomberg, 2026/10/2(上圖)、GS, 2026/9/1(下圖)

➢ 此利差通常在升息初期上升至相對高點，隨後隨著政
策利率的提升，利差逐步下滑，因此不排除短期十年
殖利率在債務疑慮下仍有小幅上升可能，但在此水準
佈局，風險承擔相對小。
➢ 儘管市場持續討論AI融資排擠美國公債需求，然而就
近期數月的財政部公布的拍賣倍數觀察，30年期維持
在2.3~2.6X之間，符合過去長期平均水準，並未出現
明顯下滑，公債需求依舊穩定。

本週股市財報訊息
18
重點提醒
➢ 本週美股將正式迎來3Q26財報季，主要由大型金融、健
護和半導體巨擘打頭陣。彭博預估，3Q26 S&P500整體
EPS年增率24.5%，其中金融年增2.5%、健護年增3.6%、
科技年增65.7%。
➢ 9月美國銀行CEO表示，Q3 BAC投行收入年減10%，交
易營收大致持平，引發股價重挫，然而花旗則釋出相對
樂觀訊號，預期公司Q3投行、交易業務仍分別維持低個
位數、中個位數成長，甚至有進一步攀升空間。Q3美股
波動度雖由Q2平均14.8%降低至12.5%，但幅度不大，
資本市場業務有望出現超預期轉機，在金融股近月股價、
估值已明顯修正下，重新推動上漲機會。
➢ 聯合健康持續關注MLR比率(醫療成本/保費收入)是否維
持下行趨勢。2026上半年此數值優於去年同期，今年7-8
月醫療保健服務平均通膨增速由Q2的平均3.2%進一步下
滑至2.6%，暗示3Q26 MLR比率有望進一步改善，將幫
助醫保類股持續修復2025年以來的跌勢。
